Welcome back to World Brief, where we’re looking at military commitments for Pakistan , Saudi Arabia , and Turkey ; a mass shooting in Thailand ; and Colombia ’s new right-wing president. Pakistan, Saudi Arabia, and Turkey inked a joint defense pact on Friday, concluding nearly a year of negotiations aimed at countering aggressive military posturing in the Middle East. Dubbed the Mecca Joint Defense Agreement, the alliance will act as a NATO-style mutual defense pact that consolidates the power and influence of three regional heavyweights. According to a trilateral statement, “the agreement is intended to strengthen collective deterrence against any act of aggression, and stipulates that any armed attack against any one of the three states shall be regarded as an attack against them all.” Although Egypt participated in early talks, Cairo was not mentioned as a participant in Friday’s deal. Pakistani Prime Minister Shehbaz Sharif, Saudi Crown Prince Mohammed bin Salman, and Turkish President Recep Tayyip Erdogan all attended the signing in Mecca, Saudi Arabia—Islam’s holiest city. Military cooperation among the three Sunni Muslim-majority nations is nothing new. For decades, Pakistan has provided Saudi forces with training and technical aid, Islamabad and Ankara have previously exchanged warships and training aircraft, and Riyadh has repeatedly purchased Turkish drones. Last month, Pakistan and Turkey also backed a Saudi-proposed maritime defense coalition that would help protect regional shipping routes and prevent global supply chains from being held hostage during war. But just how far the joint defense pact will go remains unclear . The three nations do not have a history of eagerly providing military support; last September, Pakistan signed a mutual defense deal with Saudi Arabia. The moment that my friend decided she wanted to leave China came one day in 2018, on a busy street in Beijing. A born-and-bred Beijinger with a well-paying job at an international company, she was riding in a taxi when she saw a man lying at a bus stop, apparently having a seizure. There are plenty of tales of strangers selflessly coming to the aid of others. But I have heard enough stories like the one told by my friend, who now lives in Washington, D.C., to say that the indifference and mistrust pervading public life play a significant role in some people’s decision to leave—even when they have privileged and prosperous lives in China. News about China’s dominance in clean energy and electric vehicles , along with its advances in artificial intelligence and robotics , can make the country sound like the obvious place for ambitious Chinese elites to thrive. Carefully selected videos of gleaming cities, efficient delivery networks, and rapidly developing industries project the image of a society hurtling toward a bright future. Trump’s war has catalyzed a reversion to a period before Great Britain and the United States enforced the neutrality of major global waterways in the 19th and early 20th centuries, a deliberately manufactured situation that modern Americans inherited without remembering or understanding that it was artificial, and like everything artificial, needed to be maintained. One exemplifies the way control over geography can make even a weak power vital to international decision-making. * An ‘Odyssey,’ From a Dog’s Perspective
  Foreign Policy | 07 Aug 18:00 UTC
  Plus, a satire about a Nigerian on holiday, and more international fiction in August.
  FULL TEXT: This month, we’re reading two deeply divergent takes on displacement and the pull of home, from an unnamed country in the wake of a devastating war to modern Nigeria. Paul Yoon (Scribner, 208 pp., $28, August 2026) This month, we’re reading two deeply divergent takes on displacement and the pull of home, from an unnamed country in the wake of a devastating war to modern Nigeria. Paul Yoon (Scribner, 208 pp., $28, August 2026) As many a moviegoer leaving Christopher Nolan’s Odyssey can attest, the most powerful scene in Homer’s epic is arguably a quiet one—when Odysseus returns home to Ithaca to find that his dog, Argos, has waited for him for 20 years. Upon seeing Odysseus, who cannot greet him lest he give up his disguise, Argos dies. (It’s also potentially the first moment of sentimentality in the Western tradition.) Dogs, Homer knew, unearth layers of emotion and pathos that even tales of war and human suffering cannot always access. In Etna , Paul Yoon has tried his hand at what Nolan has called “the ultimate dog story,” reimagining the Odyssey from a canine perspective. The result is as moving and wrenching as one might expect. The novel is narrated by an ex-military dog named Etna, who was trained by Americans fighting in an unnamed Asian country as a bomb-detection and attack canine. Although the side he fought for has technically won, the country is in ruins after four years of war. As Etna sets out to attempt to find his way back to the coast he was taken from as a pup—the only reference point he has for home—he confronts what is left of this land: rubble, starvation, desolation. Few buildings still stand. Medical aid never arrives from the West. As one of the two surviving U.S. soldiers in Etna’s unit says early on, “Yeah, we sort of made a mess of things here, didn’t we?” Etna features the spare yet emotionally rich prose of Yoon’s earlier work, along with his preoccupations with trauma and displacement. (Reading this book, I was reminded often of “ Valley of the Moon ,” a gorgeous story of his in the New Yorker a few years ago.) This lack of embellishment makes Etna’s recollections of the war all the more haunting. At one point, he recalls seeing his military handler die in an explosion: “the spaces appearing between parts of his body where there shouldn’t have been spaces.” It almost feels like a paradox for a writer of such economy to have penned a wartime epic. And yet, in just 208 pages, Yoon’s novel manages to touch seemingly infinite dime
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/08/07/international-fiction-releases-etna-paul-yoon-whyteface-a-igoni-barrett/

* Latin America’s Second Pink Tide Recedes
  Foreign Policy | 07 Aug 12:00 UTC
  The latest leftward shift was shorter-lived and less accomplished than its predecessor.
  FULL TEXT: Welcome back to Foreign Policy ’s Latin America Brief. The highlights this week: The region’s so-called second pink tide comes to an end, Bolivia secures a $1.9 billion IMF loan , and the United States ends deportation protections for Haitians . Welcome back to Foreign Policy ’s Latin America Brief. The highlights this week: The region’s so-called second pink tide comes to an end, Bolivia secures a $1.9 billion IMF loan , and the United States ends deportation protections for Haitians . Right-wing lawyer Abelardo de la Espriella will be inaugurated as Colombia’s president on Friday. He replaces Gustavo Petro, the latest in a string of left-wing Latin American leaders to leave office amid the region’s broader shift toward the right. When Petro and his ideological allies were elected in the wake of the COVID-19 pandemic, their victories were so widespread that many observers dubbed the phenomenon a “second pink tide,” recalling the wave of left-leaning and socialist governments that swept through Latin America from the late 1990s to the early 2010s. The first pink tide transformed the domestic and foreign policies of many South American countries, which increasingly turned toward one another to pursue a shared agenda of regional integration. These changes were made possible in large part by a global commodities boom that increased public revenue across the region. Social spending generally went up, income inequality declined , and access to higher education expanded . By the end of the first pink tide, South American governments were cooperating regionally on health , infrastructure , and conflict resolution . That momentum gave way in the mid-2010s as falling commodity prices, mounting economic challenges, and high-profile corruption scandals ushered in a more volatile political period. Across the region’s democracies, voters elected a new crop of right-wing leaders whose foreign policies deprioritized regional integration . Their tenures, however, coincided with COVID-19, and many were ousted after a single term. That political shift gave rise to the second pink tide. By 2023, leftist governments had taken office in Argentina, Bolivia, Brazil, Chile, Colombia, and Peru, pledging to reduce inequality at home and resurrect regional integration as a central pillar of their foreign policies. (Mexico has also been led by the leftist Morena party since 2018, though it has prioritized integration with North America over South America.) Latin America’s second pink
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/08/07/latin-america-second-pink-tide-legacy-left-wing-leaders/

* Labor Market Shifts Into Reverse as Employers Balk at Hiring
  NYT Economy | 07 Aug 18:35 UTC
  A spring surge rapidly faded as the summer brought higher prices and more uncertainty for businesses, even as they avoided layoffs.
  FULL TEXT: The labor market posted a modest loss in July, as businesses shed jobs in the face of renewed tariffs and higher costs driven by war in the Middle East. Employers cut 23,000 positions on a seasonally adjusted basis last month, the Labor Department reported on Friday, and the unemployment rate dropped slightly to 4.1 percent as hundreds of thousands of people left the labor force. The lower-than-expected reading comes after what had appeared to be a surge in job creation in March and April, born of optimism around tax cuts, a respite from new tariffs, easing interest rates and lower inflation. Since then, costs have jumped as oil shipments from the Persian Gulf have remained stalled, while the Trump administration has renewed its battle against imports. Job gains for April and May were revised down by 103,000, bringing the monthly average for 2026 to 60,000. “We have a labor market that’s stable but stuck in second gear,” said Lydia Boussour, a senior economist at the consulting firm EY-Parthenon. “We still have an environment where those supply shocks are working their way through the economy, and a lot of uncertainty, and that will keep businesses cautious in hiring.” The slack hiring comes while layoffs have remained consistently low, corporate profits are high and consumer spending has held up remarkably well even as expensive gasoline weighs on wallets. Job openings, the rate of hiring and the share of people quitting their jobs have stopped falling . Those indicators suggest that companies are not looking to slash payrolls at the moment. * Weak Jobs Report Does Not Eliminate Prospects of Interest Rate Rise
  NYT Economy | 07 Aug 14:24 UTC
  Officials at the Federal Reserve are chiefly focused on the trajectory of inflation after five years of overshooting the central bank’s 2 percent target.
  FULL TEXT: July’s jobs report has reduced some of the urgency around the need for the Federal Reserve to raise interest rates, but it has not eliminated the possibility. Officials at the central bank instead appear much more sensitive to how price pressures are evolving, with heightened focus on next week’s inflation report. Officials at the Fed have maintained for months that the labor market is not a primary driver of inflation, but that it is instead fueled by surging energy prices because of the war with Iran and other supply-related shocks. July’s jobs report made that abundantly clear. Employers shed 23,000 jobs for the month, and the past two months of jobs growth were sharply revised down. More people exited the work force as well, helping to drag down the unemployment rate to 4.1 percent from 4.2 percent. And wage growth remained subdued. The combination suggests that the economy is not on as strong a footing as many have perceived it to be, which helps the case that rate increases from the Fed are not immediately necessary. But for that position to strengthen, the inflation data would need to cooperate. Officials have grown increasingly impatient about the lack of progress toward the central bank’s 2 percent target. The Fed has overshot that level for half a decade and has moved further away from it over the past year, not only because of the Iran war, but also President Trump’s tariffs and other factors. That impatience has been reinforced by a pledge from Kevin M. Warsh, the new chairman, to make delivering price stability the primary focus of his tenure. * Colossal Biosciences Said It Cloned Red Wolves. Is It for Real?
  Longreads | 07 Aug 21:10 UTC
  "The red wolf has long been a contentious species. The debate over its preservation got even messier last year, when Colossal said it had cloned the animal."
  FULL TEXT: Midway through Boyce Upholt’s mind-boggling dispatch, one of his sources sums up the central challenge of the Gulf Coast canid: “Why is there not just a simple explanation for what this thing is?” The “wolfy coyotes” of southeastern Texas hold genetic traces that may link them to red wolves, which were declared extinct in the wild in 1980. But a lack of data makes it challenging to define the species—or, for that matter, to determine whether a private company has cloned the red wolf or something else entirely. Upholt doesn’t deal in simple explanations; the pleasure, here, is in the complexity. The concept of a “species” is less sturdy than your high school biology teacher might have led you to believe. The most familiar definition is that a species consists of animals that can produce fertile offspring. But that’s a rule various species of canids violate all the time; it’s long been clear that North America’s soup of Canis genes is something less like a family tree and more like a river—one that’s broken by islands and sandbars into many braided channels that split and merge and re-split. “Technologies of preserving and reviving organisms are already redefining the meaning of life, death, and extinction itself.” “Are efforts to resurrect the northern white rhino more technological hubris than genuine conservation?” “How climate disasters, human sprawl—and now Trump—are coming for America’s largest salamander.”
  https://longreads.com/2026/08/07/texas-red-wolves-coyotes-cloning/

* A New Chapter in the Egypt-Turkey Story Begins at the Stadium
  New Lines | 07 Aug 16:33 UTC
  The Egyptian star’s blockbuster move to Turkey has become more than a soccer story, offering perhaps the clearest sign yet of the remarkable normalization between two regional rivals. The post A New Chapter in the Egypt-Turkey Story Begins at the Stadium appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: (Photo by: Seyit Ahmet Eksik/Anadolu via Getty Images) As Egyptian soccer player Mohamed Salah arrived in the Turkish city of Trabzon this week, thousands of supporters of its Trabzonspor sports club flooded the airport in the team’s colors, chanting his name as they celebrated the arrival of the star, who had cemented his name as one of Liverpool’s legends after 10 years in the English Premier League. Later, they packed the club’s stadium, setting off flares and fireworks and holding up giant banners as Salah smiled back and led the crowd in chants, even unveiling what fans are already speculating could become his new trademark goal celebration. It was a reception befitting a legend who transcends soccer. For a player who has spent nearly 15 years abroad playing for Basel, Chelsea, Rome and, most recently, Liverpool, his move to the Turkish club makes him the marquee star in a Muslim-majority country for the first time since leaving his native Egypt in 2012. A decade ago, the story of an Egyptian soccer icon moving to Turkey would have unfolded against one of the Middle East’s most bitter of political feuds. Today, things look different. For much of the past decade, Egypt and Turkey stood on opposite sides of nearly every regional fault line. Turkish President Recep Tayyip Erdoğan fiercely condemned Abdel Fattah el-Sisi’s overthrow of Mohamed Morsi, while Egypt viewed Turkey as the chief patron of the Muslim Brotherhood. Their rivalry spilled into Libya and the eastern Mediterranean, resurfacing at nearly every regional summit and appearing at times to threaten confrontation between the two countries. But that has changed remarkably quickly. Since 2024, Erdoğan has visited Cairo, and Sisi has traveled to Ankara, as the two governments have signed more than a dozen cooperation agreements, expanding trade and investment and even security coordination. Though they still disagree over several issues, including some related to Libya, Sudan and Syria, the relationship has shifted from ideological rivalry to pragmatic management. In this context, Salah’s transfer appears to be one of the clearest cultural manifestations of a wider geopolitical realignment already underway. That shift was visible online almost immediately. The announcement unleashed a wave of mutual celebration that would have been difficult to imagine only a few years ago. * The Odyssey Wars
  New Lines | 07 Aug 10:26 UTC
  Nolan's “Odyssey” extracts a universal message from Homer's ancient text and laments a world where rules have collapsed and might makes right. It is a thrilling spectacle and a repudiation of the right’s culture wars. The post The Odyssey Wars appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: … There, in her head of ebony, there was no real need for the historian’s remorse, nor for literature’s. Why not see Helen as the sun saw her, with no Homeric shadow, swinging her plastic sandals on that beach alone, as fresh as the sea-wind? … In the Hindu Kush highlands where I was born, there is no word for beggar. The itinerant mendicants who walk the valley are called “mustafer,” which is Khowar for “traveler.” If one turns up at your door, you offer them food and shelter. The travelers rarely tax your generosity. The same courtesy is extended to any stranger, regardless of standing. The Pashtuns of Pakistan and Afghanistan also have the same tradition. Every Pashtun household with means has a “hujra,” usually a large room where any guest — known or unknown — is offered hospitality. The guests in turn honor the tradition by offering hospitality to others, who may be strangers to them. The tradition is sustained by paying it forward. Ancient Greeks would recognize this tradition. They called it “xenia.” The word is derived from “xenos,” the Greek word for “stranger.” Hospitality to strangers was understood as both a moral obligation and service to the gods. Zeus and Athena were believed to be protectors of strangers. The tradition obliged the host to provide hospitality and the guest to show courtesy and not to abuse the host’s generosity. The tradition was sustained by the same combination of reciprocity and paying forward. Such traditions have all but disappeared from the West. There are individual acts of generosity, but nothing that is sustained by a shared code. Indeed, over the past decade, the stranger has become a subject of hostility and derision — and not just for the far right. Before his defenestration, former U.K. Prime Minister Keir Starmer had mined xenophobia for political gain by lamenting Britain’s decline into an “island of strangers.” The word “xenophobia” is derived from the same root as “xenia.” In adapting Homer’s epic “The Odyssey,” Christopher Nolan has chosen to use xenia as the moral pivot of the story. Except that Nolan broadens it into what he calls “Zeus’ law,” which he equates to the golden rule: Treat others as you’d have them treat you. In doing so, he makes the principle universal, accessible to more than an exclusive club of classicists, while also making the epic’s final irruption of violence less incomprehensible to a modern audience. Ancient Greek traditions of hospitality may not be familiar to the average cinemag
  https://newlinesmag.com/argument/the-odyssey-wars/

On March 26, 2013, a military force from the 61st Brigade of Bashar al-Assad’s army stormed the village of Saida al-Golan (also known as Saida al-Hanout) in Quneitra governorate in southern Syria, striking terror into the residents of the small village and its surroundings. Although local sources disagreed at the time about the exact number of victims, all confirmed that a “massacre” had taken place, witnessed by residents of the village that lies just over a mile east of the buffer zone with the Israeli-occupied Golan Heights. Those sources also confirmed that regime forces had thrown the dead bodies into a nearby valley. From that point on, as the regime’s military campaigns intensified across the country, and as its forces and allies committed dozens of documented massacres, the massacre at Wadi Tuaim was set aside. Neither the victims nor their families were granted any measure of justice against the perpetrators who had killed their sons. But the fall of the Assad regime has finally given them the chance to raise their voices and demand the justice they have sought for 13 years, and to tell a story whose details they whispered among themselves for fear of the deposed regime’s retribution. This investigation by SIRAJ reveals for the first time the names and faces of two officers of the former regime’s army accused of being responsible for the Wadi Tuaim massacre, in which 13 people have been confirmed killed, nine of them from a single family, including a child.   FULL TEXT: Governments that deploy internet shutdowns to thwart protesters have struggled to clamp down on offline messaging apps. India’s recent response to Jack Dorsey’s Bitchat suggests that battle is entering a new phase. Bitchat, launched by the Twitter co-founder in 2025, allows nearby phones to communicate over Bluetooth even when mobile networks or internet access are unavailable. Last month, the app had its biggest real-world test after authorities shut down internet access during student protests in New Delhi. As protesters turned to Bitchat, the Indian government tried to block access to the app’s source code on GitHub, a Microsoft-owned platform that allows developers to create, store, manage, and share their code. In the past, the Indian government has blocked apps from official app stores due to various reasons, but digital rights advocates say this appears to be the first known attempt to geoblock an open-source software repository. The episode highlights a growing challenge for governments. Across the globe, state-enforced internet blackouts have become a primary weapon against protests and independent journalism during political crises. Beyond silencing dissent, these measures serve to block real-time documentation and evade international scrutiny of official misconduct. Apps built to function without the internet make those tactics less effective. “The decentralized architecture of the app means that there is no central server or database and therefore there cannot be a central kill switch,” Namrata Maheshwari, senior policy counsel and encryption policy lead at digital civil rights nonprofit Access Now, told Rest of World . “Downloaded apps will continue to work as long as they are within the physical proximity required for the mesh network to function.” Bitchat reached an all-time high of 430,000 daily active users in India on July 26, according to market intelligence firm Sensor Tower. Between July 20 and July 26, India accounted for 74% of the app’s global downloads. During Hong Kong’s 2014 protests, FireChat became a popular way for demonstrators to communicate without mobile networks. More recently, Bitchat downloads spiked during protests in Myanmar, Nepal, Uganda, Madagascar, and Iran. The app also reached the top of Apple’s App Store in Jamaica amid power outages during a hurricane last year. India’s response, however, went beyond targeting the app itself.   FULL TEXT: At the turn of the millennium, India became the global hub for medical transcription. With fiber optic cables laid in the 1990s, American hospitals found it cheap and efficient to beam voice files across the ocean. Indian workers, fluent in English and medical terminology, turned dictations into polished reports overnight. The time-zone gap became an advantage: a surgeon in Boston could dictate before bed, and wake up to flawless notes typed in Bengaluru. By the early 2000s, the industry seemed secure. Companies built training academies, invested in IT systems and promised young recruits that this was a career with a future. But when I met Aakash (name changed) in June 2024, that promise had already unravelled. At 25, he was clinging to a short-term contract in a Bengaluru firm. He had joined a year earlier, after months of training. During recruitment, he asked the CEO whether AI posed a threat. ‘At least five years away,’ came the reassurance. Yet, by March 2024, as the financial year closed, American clients began to vanish. Contracts were cancelled or shifted to automated platforms. Hundreds of jobs disappeared almost overnight. The cafeteria that once buzzed at 3 a.m. emptied out. Hiring froze; the recruitment team itself was laid off. New employees were brought in under “conditional retention training,” only to be dismissed weeks later (an accounting trick to show inflated headcounts to prospective clients) and workers lived in limbo. Economists have a phrase for jobs like Aakash’s: high exposure , low complementarity. Which means that these are roles where machines can easily take over, rather than raising the human’s capacity to work. In Manila, tens of thousands of Filipino transcriptionists have already been displaced . In Nairobi, call center operators now compete with chatbots for contracts that once fed families. In Colombia, customer support roles vanish into generative systems. The pattern is the same: codified knowledge that once built middle-class livelihoods is being swallowed whole by automation. Even the people training the systems feel the blade at their neck. Contractors working on products like Gemini and AI Overviews describe a familiar dread: they are annotating their own obsolescence. Unlike earlier waves of mechanization that chipped away at manual labour, these systems target the cognitive core of professional life. The scale of this change is huge. Even jobs that once seemed safe because of their creativity or complexity are no
  https://restofworld.org/2026/ai-jobs-economy-impact/?utm_source=rss&utm_medium=rss&utm_campaign=feeds

* A Haitian American Reflects on the Future

The post A Haitian American Reflects on the Future...
  FULL TEXT: Jan. 13, 2010, is a day that I will never forget. That Wednesday morning, I had several missed calls, text messages and notifications on my phone. I did not know it at the time, but the day before, a massive earthquake had struck near the capital city of Haiti, Port-au-Prince. As the child of Haitian immigrants who came to the United States in 1980, I have always kept up with events in Haiti, and this was, without question, the most devastating natural disaster that had occurred on the island in my lifetime. Hundreds of thousands of Haitians died as a result of the magnitude 7 earthquake, and millions more were severely impacted. At that point, I didn’t know all this — I was concerned for my family. Many of my cousins, uncles and aunts were still in Haiti. Were they okay? What would they do now that the capital had been damaged so severely? Where would they find refuge? On Jan. 15, in the midst of the chaos and confusion, the United States government extended temporary protected status (TPS) to Haitian nationals, giving them the ability to live and work in the United States without fear of deportation. Now, after 15 years of Haitians building their lives, integrating into their local communities and contributing to the economic and social fabric of the United States, the second Trump administration has abruptly ended TPS for Haiti and has plunged communities across the nation into a state of fear, anxiety and despair. Our industries, our hospitals, our nursing homes, our businesses and the entire U.S. economy will be harmed by this decision. TPS was established with the passage of the Immigration Act of 1990. The law significantly reshaped our immigration system and provided additional pathways for immigrants to come to the U.S. It raised the number of immigrants allowed to enter each year from 290,000 to 675,000 and introduced various programs, such as the Diversity Visa lottery and the H-1B Skilled Worker Visa Program, to increase the number of diverse, skilled workers entering the country. Along with these changes to the U.S. immigration system, this law established the TPS program, which, in this instance, provided work permits and protection for Haitians living in the U.S. after the earthquake. TPS designations are applied to countries that are experiencing extremely challenging circumstances, such as “on-going armed conflict, an environmental disaster, or extraordinary and temporary conditions,” as stated by the U.S. Department of Homeland Security.

* Magical Mystery Toads
  Longreads | 04 Aug 10:00 UTC
  The long, tangled, and mostly wrong history of the psychedelic toad.
  FULL TEXT: Kimon de Greef | Longreads | August 4, 2026 | 4,741 words (17 minutes) From The Ego Trip: Psychedelic Toads, a Trail of Deaths, and the Guru Who Peddled Transcendence by Kimon de Greef. Copyright © 2026 by Kimon de Greef. Excerpted by permission of Doubleday, an imprint of The Knopf Doubleday Group, a division of Penguin Random House LLC. All rights reserved. In 1935, two chemists at Tokyo’s Imperial University synthesized a new molecule and unwittingly launched one of the strangest narratives of the psychedelic age. Toshio Hoshino and Kenya Shimodaira were working on a family of compounds known as the tryptamines, which occur widely in nature and have a range of intriguing biological properties. The neurotransmitter serotonin, which among other things regulates mood, cognition, and social behavior, is a tryptamine. So are many psychoactive drugs, although this would only be discovered later on. The Japanese scientists had recently completed the world’s first laboratory synthesis of a molecule called bufotenine, which is produced by toads and certain species of mushrooms. Now, via a sequence of reactions, they engineered a close chemical relative. Diagrams of the two tryptamines looked nearly identical, each represented by two angular rings and a tail, resembling microscopic organisms; the only difference lay in the shape of the head. Such tiny changes in chemical structure “can lead to radically different consequences, like a railroad’s switch points,” as the writer and organic chemist Primo Levi once wrote. The new molecule’s name, following scientific convention, would be 5-MeO-DMT. Hoshino and Shimodaira published their findings in the Bulletin of the Chemical Society of Japan, leaving no explanation as to why they had conducted these experiments. The early 20th century was a time of wide-ranging exploration in organic chemistry, with researchers synthesizing new compounds to assess their pharmacological potential or better understand the reactions by which one substance could be transformed into another. Many of these compounds had no evident utility, such as 5-MeO-DMT, which for decades was thought only to be a product of the laboratory. This changed in 1959, when the molecule was isolated from a shrub native to Brazil. Not long afterward, 5-MeO-DMT was detected in Anadenanthera peregrina, a leguminous tree from the Caribbean and South America. For thousands of years, Indigenous communities had been harvesting its slender bean pods to prepare snuffs

The people who brought the refined morphine could have been Armenian, they could have been Lebanese, most likely Lebanese Christians. And the people who actually brought him the actual heroin, the dope, most likely would’ve been French.” Gingeras and Al Yafai’s discussion begins with American mafia culture, but expands both geographically and thematically, taking in how networks like those depicted on screen helped to build and shape the modern nations in which they now operate. Gingeras himself, an expert in Ottoman history, came to realize the personal impact of such networks when travelling to Turkey for work. “It took me leaving not so much New York, but leaving the United States and coming to Turkey to figure out that this was something that was a bigger story,” Gingeras says. “People all over the world struggled with this idea of, ‘What does the world mean to me when mafias are such an important part of it?’ In New York, it’s second nature, but what we, especially in the United States, oftentimes fail to really grasp is that this is something people struggle with everywhere.” Despite the fact they operate in the underworld, mafias matter to people everywhere, Gingeras argues, because they bring into relief the blurred edges of the nation-state. “Mafias are people with faces,” Gingeras says.

* Why Silicon Valley is divided over China’s powerful, cheap AI models
  Rest of World | 03 Aug 10:00 UTC
  Low-cost Chinese open-weight models are splitting tech executives and Washington over national security and competitiveness.
  FULL TEXT: The surge of powerful Chinese open-weight AI models has sparked a feud in Silicon Valley and Washington over whether they are threats to U.S. national security or essential parts of the expanding AI economy. Entrepreneurs and politicians are taking sides — arguing for either unfettered access or a strict crackdown — by forming alliances, issuing open letters, and launching social media volleys. While the leading American AI companies have kept their best models closed-source, Chinese AI labs have been releasing open-weight models as a way to attract global customers. These open models are becoming increasingly powerful. Moonshot’s open-weight model, Kimi K3, now ranks fourth on Artificial Analysis’ intelligence index , behind only Anthropic’s Opus 5 and Fable 5 and OpenAI’s GPT-5.6 Sol. While the adoption of Chinese models is on the rise in the U.S., politicians and companies have also sounded alarms. Some accuse Chinese companies of building powerful models with smuggled chips and distillation — using the output of American models to train their own. Others warn of the security risks posed by the powerful, free-to-download models. Whether to embrace or reject these Chinese models has become a divisive issue in the U.S. tech industry. Chinese open models are cost-effective. Open-weight models are free to download, but running them incurs computing costs. Many users pay to access the models through their developers or third-party cloud providers. Facing ballooning AI costs, some U.S. companies and developers have been diverting simpler tasks to Chinese models to save money. Companies can also use Chinese open-weight models to build customized AI systems, or host the models locally so they don’t have to share data with external parties. Chinese open models benefit AI infrastructure providers and companies that need affordable AI services. Nvidia has been a leading voice in support of open-weight models. The availability of open models creates a bigger customer base for its AI chips. On July 24, Nvidia chief executive Jensen Huang posted on X for the first time to share an open letter stressing the importance of open models in expanding access to the AI economy. Other tech giants, including Microsoft, Google, and Meta, have since joined the letter. OpenAI, which has released open-weight models that are smaller than China’s models, also signed on. “i want the US to win in AI both in open source and proprietary models,” chief executive Sam Altman wrote in reply
